🟢 **The supply-side findings in this pass are a different tier entirely — byte-read grants, not
citations** (`agents/top.md`, `repos/foundations.md`, `p852`).

### 🔴 The region placement this pass could and could NOT make

🔴 **`13` permissive repositories were graded this pass. `2` carry payload evidence of a region;
`11` do not.** 🟢 **Placed, and on what channel:**

| Row | Region | The channel that placed it |
|---|---|---|
| `UOC/java-lti-1.3` | **EMEA** | 🟢 Maintainer contact in the repository is `xaracil at uoc dot edu` — a stated institutional domain (Universitat Oberta de Catalunya, Spain) |
| `TREMA-UNH/rubric-grading-workbench` | **North America** | 🟡 The ACCOUNT name carries an institutional acronym (`-UNH`, University of New Hampshire) and the grant holder is that group's lead — account-name evidence, not a resolved domain |

🔴 **NOT placed, and the reason, stated once per class:** `webtech-network/autograder` renders
reports in Brazilian Portuguese — 🔵 **that is a LOCALISATION CAPABILITY and this base does not
read a capability as an origin**, so it is the LATAM-facing default in the adapter layer without
being a LATAM artefact. `EscolaLMS/LRS` and `Citolab/qti-scoring-engine` carry suggestive names
(a Polish-language author field; a plausible link to the Dutch institute Cito) and **state no
organisation or country**, so no region is filed — a name is not a channel (`T165`).
🔴 **`11` of `13` unplaced is the real coverage number for this pass's supply side.** → `852-G6`.

🔵 **And one region-hygiene defect in the SOURCES, recorded because it is the exact failure this
file's closed vocabulary exists to prevent:** the APAC leg
(`AI education APAC 2026 adoption regulation players`) returned, as an APAC regulatory fact, a
**UK** generative-AI schools standard dated January 2026. 🔴 **The UK is EMEA.** 🟢 **Corrected on
intake rather than carried; the item is cited under EMEA below and flagged as single-source.**

### 🟢 Demand side — what the four legs returned, by region

🔵 **Global, and the two numbers worth quoting to a client.** Microsoft's 2025 reporting puts
generative-AI use among education organisations at **`86%` — the highest of any industry it
measured**; RAND finds the share of K-12 teachers using generative AI for their own work roughly
**doubled** between the 2023-24 and 2024-25 school years. 🔴 **The number that should temper both:
only `45%` of the most-used tools have published research meeting an **ESSA evidence tier**.**
🟡 **OECD's Digital Education Outlook 2026 states the constraint plainly — AI helps learning only
when pedagogy stays in control.** 🔴 **Market sizing is irreconcilable across sources and should
not be quoted as a single figure:** `~$10.6B` (2026) rising to `$42.48B` (2030) from one house,
`$41.01B` for 2030 from another, against a `$238.4B` projection for EdTech overall in 2026.
🟢 **Fading, per an edtech trend review: blockchain credentialing, full-classroom VR, and the
chatbot-as-tutor pitch** — 🔵 **note that the third of those is the category most of this base's
tutor shelf sits in, which is an argument for the marking and standards tiers filed this pass.**

🔵 **North America.** The largest market by revenue (one vendor puts its share at `~38%`; Technavio
frames North America as `~41.7%` of global *growth* 2026-2030 — different measures, not
interchangeable). Student use is reported climbing from `66%` (2024) to `92%` (2025), with
institution-wide implementation at `66%`. 🔴 **The demand signal with teeth is legislative, not
commercial:** federal executive orders through early 2026 produced a national framework and a
K-12 AI instruction task force, the PIE Network tracked **~`100` state bills** this year touching
students' AI use, and **at least `15` states** have introduced or passed AI-in-education
legislation; Virginia's S.B. 394 directs the state agency to issue guidance including teacher
training. A California ballot initiative announced January 2026 could change how AI companies
interact with minors. 🟢 **Procurement-relevant:** the US Department of Education released
`$169M` in FIPSE awards that include responsible-AI use, and Instructure's **Canvas Career**
(skills-first AI LMS for workforce learning) reached GA in January 2026. 🔴 **Chegg cut `45%` of
its staff as AI reduced homework-help traffic — the clearest market evidence that the
answer-retrieval layer is gone, not disrupted.** 🔴 **Declared gap: no authoritative source on
Canada or Mexico was retrievable, so "North America" here means the United States.**

🔵 **EMEA.** 🟢 **The single most decision-relevant fact in this file this pass:** political
agreement on the **Digital Omnibus** simplification package on **7 May 2026** pushes the AI Act's
**high-risk obligations for education to 2 December 2027**. 🔴 **Education remains a high-risk
domain; the date moved, the classification did not.** The Commission published draft guidelines on
classifying high-risk systems on **19 May 2026** with consultation to **23 June 2026**, and EU
education ministers approved conclusions on teachers in the age of AI on **11 May 2026**, calling
for AI literacy and education-specific tools. Eurydice's *Digital education at school in Europe
2026* is the reference text. 🟡 **ISO/IEC 42001 is cited as the operational complement to the
policy layer** — the thing a studio can actually certify against while the Act's dates move.
🔴 **Teacher sentiment is the delivery risk:** an Epson-commissioned survey of ~`3,360` people
across the UK, France, Italy, Germany, Spain and Poland found **`80%` of teachers worry AI is
entering classrooms faster than schools can manage.** 🟡 **Single-source, flagged: a UK
generative-AI safety standard for schools dated January 2026** (returned mis-bucketed under the
APAC leg; verify against the ministry before use). 🔴 **Declared gap: nothing retrievable on
vendor market share in EMEA, or on national rules outside the EU.**

🔵 **APAC.** 🟡 **The fastest-expanding region by most accounts, and the least legible.** Times
Higher Education (April 2026) describes **patchy** uptake with limited connectivity as a probable
barrier and puts virtual-tutor adoption at `30%` of its surveyed cohort; a market house reports a
`50%` increase in AI-based learning-platform adoption over two years and describes the market as
fragmented between agile startups and incumbents. 🟢 **Regulation is national and restrictive
where it exists:** the UAE Ministry of Education is reported to have issued **`25` generative-AI
prohibitions for schools in February 2026** covering age suitability, privacy, academic integrity
and exam restrictions 🟡 **(single blog source — verify against the ministry)**. 🔵 **Public
opinion runs the other way: the Ipsos Education Monitor 2026 finds LOWER support for banning AI in
schools across the Asian markets it examined** — a combination (restrictive rules, permissive
publics) that does not hold in EMEA and is worth knowing before pitching. Capacity-building is
real: **`34,000` lead teachers** are being trained through 2026 for an AI classroom rollout.
Convenings: Acer's APAC summit and Cambridge English's Kuala Lumpur leaders' meeting, both April
2026. 🔴 **Declared gap: no consolidated player list and no single APAC regulatory framework was
retrievable; the region is a set of national markets and must be quoted that way.**

🔵 **LATAM.** 🟢 **The highest reported engagement of any region in this file, and the widest
gap between use and governance.** The Digital Education Council's 2026 LATAM survey (run with
Tecnológico de Monterrey's Institute for the Future of Education, `29` institutions) reports
**`92%` of students and `79%` of faculty actively engaging with AI**, with a weekly-use figure of
`67%` among students. 🔴 **Against that: only ~`30%` of Latin American universities have published
policies on how to use AI.** 🟡 **A separate `30%` figure — the share who believe their
institution responded effectively — circulates alongside it; they are different measures and must
not be conflated.** UNESCO IESALC's September 2026 working paper (with UNU-IAS) maps AI use across
teaching, research, community engagement, administration and governance, and its regional survey
covers **`200` institutions in `19` countries**, finding the adoption-versus-governance gap
"particularly pronounced" in the region. Times Higher Education reports adoption "neither uniform
nor evenly distributed", with the split running between private and public universities.
🔴 **Intelligent CIO reports the region's AI inequality widening on BOTH compute and adoption —
the structural constraint behind every number above.** 🟢 **Active institutions, which is what a
studio actually needs:** UNESCO IESALC · Digital Education Council with Tecnológico de Monterrey ·
**UNESCO with Chile's CENIA** (ethical-AI agreement, including Latam-GPT integration) ·
**EdTech Hub with Fundación Ceibal** (partnership launched September 2026) · the IDB · ECLAC's
eLAC digital agenda. 🟡 **Latam-GPT, measured as carefully as the sources allow:** launched
**10 February 2026** by CENIA, reported as a `70B`-parameter model built on Llama 3.1 (an earlier
2025 source says `50B` — different stages), at a cost around `$550K`, with institutional counts
that disagree across sources (`30+` institutions / `8` countries versus `60+` / `15`), and stated
to be free for companies and public institutions. 🔴 **No GitHub repository for Latam-GPT was
retrievable from these results, so this base still has NO byte-read grant for it and cannot place
it on a shelf — the fifth consecutive pass in which that is true.** → `852-G7`.

## Opportunities by region — as filed by `p852` (historical, superseded above)

🟢 **Each subsection names what was SHELVED this pass that the region can be sold on, and refuses
to name an opportunity this base cannot supply.**

### North America

🟢 **The sellable thing is assessment EVIDENCE, not assessment automation.** The regulatory
pressure in this region is legislative and evidentiary — `~100` state bills, `≥15` states with
enacted or introduced law, and only `45%` of the most-used tools carrying ESSA-tier research.
🔵 **`TREMA-UNH/rubric-grading-workbench` (MIT, `15★`, filed this pass) is the only permissive
thing on these shelves that produces an inter-annotator agreement number between an AI grader and
human graders** — which is precisely the artefact an ESSA-tier claim or a state procurement
question demands. 🟢 **Compose it with `webtech-network/autograder` (Apache-2.0, sandboxed) and
you have a marking pilot that can defend its own accuracy.** → `P81`.
🔴 **Do NOT lead with FERPA tooling.** The one permissive Canvas adapter found
(`jlouisbru/Canvas-grading-with-AI`) advertises a "FERPA-compliant workflow" with **no independent
audit**, and student answer text does reach the model API. 🟢 **Sell the de-identification
pattern; never repeat the compliance word.**

### EMEA

🟢 **The sellable thing is the AI Act clock, and it is now a build window rather than an
emergency.** High-risk education obligations moved to **2 December 2027** under the Digital
Omnibus agreement of 7 May 2026 — 🔵 **roughly `13` months of runway from today, against a
classification that did not change.** 🟢 **This pass put the EMEA-anchored seam row on the shelf:
`UOC/java-lti-1.3` (MIT, `21★`, Universitat Oberta de Catalunya), a full LTI 1.3 Advantage tool on
the JVM** — the stack most European public-sector education IT is procured in. 🟢 **Pair it with
`metyatech/qti-scoring-system` (MIT), whose marker comments round-trip into QTI Results Reporting
`COMMENT` outcomes and which ships axe accessibility tests** — 🔵 **accessibility being a
procurement requirement in this region rather than a nice-to-have, and ISO/IEC 42001 the frame the
whole thing can be certified against.** → `P82`.
🔴 **The honest blocker: `open-ent/openbadges` — the French Open ENT credential issuer, the single
most relevant EMEA credential finding of the pass — carries NO grant file and cannot be used.**

### APAC

🔴 **This base cannot currently serve an APAC engagement as well as it serves EMEA or North
America, and this pass states that rather than implying parity.** 🟢 **What is real:** the
restrictive-rules / permissive-publics combination (UAE's `25` prohibitions in February 2026
against Ipsos finding low support for school AI bans) makes **auditable, locally-run marking** the
right pitch — every row in this pass's marking tier runs on-premise, and
`BridgeSuite/GradeBridge-Assignment-Maker` (MIT) authors assessments **entirely in the browser with
no backend and no telemetry**, which is the posture an exam-restriction rule is satisfied by.
🔵 **`34,000` teachers in training through 2026 is a teacher-enablement demand signal, not a
platform one.** 🔴 **Declared gap, unchanged: this pass shelved `0` rows with APAC payload
evidence, and no consolidated APAC regulatory framework or player list was retrievable. Any APAC
proposal is built on globally-sourced components placed by capability, not by region.**

### LATAM

🟢 **The sellable thing is the governance gap, and it is measured: `92%` student engagement
against `~30%` of universities having any published AI-use policy.** 🔵 **That is a policy-and-
instrumentation engagement, not a tooling one** — the client needs a defensible marking process
before it needs a better marker. 🟢 **What this pass can supply for it:**
`webtech-network/autograder` (Apache-2.0, `66★`) is the natural anchor because it **renders
reports in Brazilian Portuguese as well as English** and sandboxes submissions — 🔴 **recorded
once more as a localisation capability, not a regional origin.** 🟢 **Compose with
`TREMA-UNH/rubric-grading-workbench` to produce the agreement evidence a public university's
governance committee will ask for.** → `P81`.
🔴 **The standing supply gap: Latam-GPT launched in February 2026 and this base still has no
retrievable repository and therefore no byte-read grant for it, five passes running. Until that
changes, a LATAM proposal that promises a regional model is promising something this base has not
verified.** → `852-G7`.

### Global

🟢 **The cross-region thesis this pass earned:** the permissive opportunity in education has moved
**from the tutor to the seam**. 🔴 **The chatbot-as-tutor pitch is named as FADING by the trend
review, and it is the category most of this base's historic tutor shelf occupies.** 🟢 **The
`6` standards-seam rows filed this pass — LTI, QTI, xAPI, all MIT, `0` copyleft — are what a
studio integrates WITH rather than competes against**, and they are licence-clean in a field where
every major platform (Moodle GPL-3.0, Open edX AGPL-3.0, Canvas AGPL-3.0, Sakai ECL-2.0) is not.
🔵 **The general rule, now stated for the thirteenth pass: you do not need a permissive LMS,
because integration code does not inherit the platform's grant.** 🟢 **`86%` of education
organisations already use generative AI; the unmet need is not adoption, it is evidence —
`45%` ESSA-tier coverage is the number that sells an evaluation harness.**

more than a larger one that cannot** — and this base's own `make absence-census`

## 🟢 Fifty-third pass (`p848`, 2026-11-16) — **`p844`'s open residual is CLOSED: the Article `50` marking grace ends `2026-12-02`, `16` days from this pass — and the sub-paragraph that bites a STUDIO was never deferred at all**

🟡 **Provenance, stated before the findings.** Every regulatory fact below is **search-tier**:
read from indexed secondary commentary via WebSearch, **`CITED, NOT READ`**. 🔴 **No primary text
was retrievable from this environment, and this pass established why — see `T162`: direct HTTP is
an allow-list that admits GitHub hosts and nothing else.** `eur-lex.europa.eu`,
`www.lexology.com`, `vietnamnews.vn`, `www.unesco.org`, `www.bakerbotts.com` and
`compliancehub.wiki` all fail identically at `rc=56` (`evidence/egress-allowlist-probe.2026-11-16-p848.tsv`).
🟢 **Do not quote these dates to a client as verified; quote them as the consensus of named
secondary readings and have counsel confirm against the Official Journal.**

### 🟢 EMEA — the residual `p844` carried is resolved, and it splits in a way that changes who is late

🔴 **`p844` recorded: *"the content-marking grace for systems already on the market is reported as
`2026-12-02` in one source against Art. `50` from `2026-08-02` in others. Quote Art. `50` as live;
quote the marking grace as contested."*** 🟢 **It is no longer contested. Both are true, because
they are different sub-paragraphs.**

| Duty | Who it binds | When it applies | State today (`2026-11-16`) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Art. `50(2)`** — machine-readable marking of synthetic output | 🔵 **Providers** | Systems placed on the EU market **on or after `2026-08-02`**: 🔴 **no grace, marking from day one** | 🔴 **LIVE** |
| **Art. `50(2)`** — same duty, legacy estate | 🔵 **Providers** | Systems placed on the market **before `2026-08-02`**: 🟢 grace until **`2026-12-02`** | 🔴 **`16` DAYS REMAIN** |
| **Art. `50(4)`** — disclosure that content is AI-generated / that the learner is interacting with AI | 🔵 **Deployers** | 🔴 **Not deferred at all** | 🔴 **LIVE since `2026-08-02` — already late** |
| Annex III stand-alone high-risk — education | Providers | Deferred by `Regulation (EU) 2026/1744` | `2027-12-02` |
| Annex I product-embedded | Providers | | `2028-08-02` |

🟢 **The commercially important line is the third one.** 🔵 **A studio delivering an education AI
system into a European institution is ordinarily a *deployer* or is building for one, and the
deployer's Art. `50(4)` disclosure duty was never in any grace period.** 🔴 **It has been live
since `2026-08-02` and is therefore already late in every running deployment** — which sharpens
rather than contradicts this file's standing `T147` advice.

🟢 **The second line is the dated one worth acting on.** 🔵 **Any generative education system a
client placed on the EU market before `2026-08-02` has until `2026-12-02` to retrofit
machine-readable marking.** 🟢 **Content generated before the cutoff does not need retroactive
labelling** — the relief is prospective, which bounds the remediation to the pipeline rather than
the archive. 🔵 **That is a two-week, scopeable engagement with a statutory deadline on it, and it
is the single most sellable compliance item in this file right now.**

🟡 **Corroboration: eight independent secondary readings** — Baker Botts, `compliancehub.wiki`,
`reptile.haus`, `intelliconnectq.com`, `highcircl.com`, `complylayer.com`, `regulation-ai.eu`,
`technology.org`. 🔴 **Residual, carried honestly:** the sources split on whether `2026-12-02` is
best described as a *grace period* or a *provider deadline*. 🟢 **They agree on the date and on the
substance**, so the disagreement is vocabulary, not law.

### 🟢 APAC — Vietnam's high-risk list is ENUMERATED, and education is `3` of `46`

🟢 **`Decision No. 33/2026/QD-TTg`, effective `2026-08-15`, lists `46` high-risk AI systems across
`6` sectors.** 🔵 **This base already held that Vietnam names education high-risk. What it did not
hold is the enumeration, and the enumeration is the usable part.**

| Sector | Systems named | Share of the `46` |
|---|---|---|
| Transport | `31` | `67%` |
| Ethnic and religious affairs | `7` | `15%` |
| 🔵 **Education** | 🔵 **`3`** | 🔵 **`7%`** |
| Healthcare | `2` | `4%` |
| Banking | `2` | `4%` |
| Judicial proceedings | `1` | `2%` |

🟢 **The three education systems, as reported:**

1. 🔴 **Self-learning content generated from uncontrolled data sources.**
2. 🔴 **Systems that automatically evaluate and rank learners.**
3. 🔴 **Systems that monitor learner behaviour using biometric data.**

🔵 **Read those as a product specification in reverse.** 🟢 **An adaptive tutor that generates its
own material from an open corpus hits (1); any placement, streaming or leaderboard feature hits
(2); any webcam-based attention or proctoring feature hits (3).** 🔵 **This base's standing
architectural lever applies directly and is now sharper: Vietnam's regime treats many listed
systems as high-risk *only where their output is the sole basis for a decision without meaningful
human review*** — so **(2) is designable around with a human-in-the-loop grade release**, while
**(1) is designable around with a curated corpus**, and 🔴 **(3) is not designable around at all —
it is a feature to decline.**

🟢 **Timetable.** Systems already operating get **`18` months** in medical, education and finance
and **`12` months** elsewhere. 🔵 **Education deadline: `2027-09-01`. Other listed sectors:
`2027-03-01`.** 🟢 **During the transition an existing system may keep operating unless a competent
State agency finds serious risk and orders suspension.** 🔴 **Discrepancy recorded, not resolved:**
sources differ on whether the `2027-09-01` date anchors to Decision 33's effective date
(`2026-08-15`) or to the Law's (`2026-03-01`), and on whether Decision 33 was signed `30 June` or
`2 July 2026`. 🟢 **This base's previously-carried derivation — "compliance by `2027-03-01`,
education systems operating before `2026-08-15` receive a further six months" — agrees with the
`2027-09-01` figure, which is independent corroboration of the date from two directions.**

### 🟢 Cross-region — the two ENUMERATED education regimes are `92` days apart, and the earlier one is not the European one

🔵 **A studio selling one adaptive-assessment product into both the EU and Vietnam now has two
dated conformity deadlines:**

| Regime | Education obligation bites | Gap |
|---|---|---|
| 🔵 **Vietnam** — Law on AI `134/2025/QH15` + Decision `33/2026/QD-TTg` | 🔴 **`2027-09-01`** | — |
| EU — AI Act Annex III as amended by `Regulation (EU) 2026/1744` | `2027-12-02` | 🔵 **`92` days later** |

🟢 **So the binding date is the Vietnamese one.** 🔵 **Most compliance planning in this market
anchors on the European calendar; a product with APAC distribution is on a clock that runs out a
quarter earlier.** 🔴 **And the two regimes converge on the same trigger — automated assessment of
learners — from completely different legal traditions**, which is the strongest argument this file
has for building assessment conformity ONCE, as a portable artefact, rather than per-jurisdiction.

### 🔴 North America, LATAM, Global — legs run, `0` new facts

🟢 **All four mandated regional legs were run.** 🔴 **Every distinctive fact the North America and
LATAM legs returned was already on this shelf**, checked with `grep` before being called new:
North Carolina **Session Law 2026-41** and its `2026-12-31` / `2027-06-30` / `2028-06-30`
milestones; the `35+` states with departmental AI guidance; the conflicting bill counts (`134`
bills / `31` states versus `71` / `27`, a counting-method difference); the US Department of
Education FIPSE awards; Universidad de los Andes' **`716` regulatory instruments across `23`
countries and territories**; **Uruguay** as the first LATAM signatory of the Council of Europe AI
treaty; Argentina's deregulatory bill; Colombia's capacity-building national policy to 2030;
Brazil's EU-modelled framework; UNESCO IESALC's **`87%` adoption against `26%` with a strategy**;
Brazil's **`53%` of administrators using AI against `22%` with guidelines**; the UNESCO regional
observatory and `Latam-GPT`. 🟢 **Held, all of it.**

🟡 **One Global figure is worth re-stating because it is the one that survives sales pressure:**
🔴 **only `45%` of the most-used education tools have published research meeting an ESSA evidence
tier.** 🔵 **In a market where the OECD's 2026 Digital Education Outlook warns that AI helps
learning only where pedagogy stays in control, an evidence package is a differentiator, not
overhead.**

## Opportunities by region — as filed by `p848` (historical, superseded above)

### North America

🟢 **Sell the deadline, not the model.** North Carolina's Session Law 2026-41 requires a **state
model policy by `2026-12-31`**, **local school-unit policies by `2027-06-30`** and **teacher
training by `2028-06-30`**, and `35+` states now publish departmental AI guidance. 🔵 **That is a
three-stage, dated procurement pipeline: policy artefact, then district-level implementation, then
training delivery at scale.** 🟢 **The studio asset that wins it is a policy-to-implementation
toolkit — a model policy mapped to concrete system controls — not a tutor.** 🔵 **Pair it with an
ESSA-tier evidence package, since federal grant priorities now favour projects demonstrating
appropriate and ethical AI use.**

### EMEA

🔴 **Two dated items, one of them `16` days away.** 🟢 **Offer (a) an Art. `50(2)` marking
retrofit for any generative education system placed on the EU market before `2026-08-02`, due
`2026-12-02`, bounded to the generation pipeline because pre-cutoff content needs no retroactive
labelling; and (b) an Art. `50(4)` deployer-disclosure remediation, which is already late and
therefore sells without a deadline argument.** 🔵 **Then position the Annex III work for
`2027-12-02` as the follow-on.** 🟢 **The sovereignty thread — the October 2026 Council text on
reducing dependence on non-European AI tools and the European Digital Education Hub — makes a
self-hosted stack (Moodle or Open edX beside locally-run models) the architecture to lead with.**

### APAC

🟢 **Vietnam is the sharpest opportunity in this file because it enumerates.** 🔵 **A registry of
a client's AI systems mapped against Decision 33's `46` entries is a deliverable that can be
produced in weeks and that every other regime in this file silently presupposes.** 🟢 **Then sell
the architectural lever: human-in-the-loop grade release moves automated learner evaluation and
ranking out of the sole-basis trigger, and a curated corpus moves generated content out of
"uncontrolled data sources".** 🔴 **Decline biometric behaviour monitoring outright — it is named,
and it is not designable around.** 🔵 **Deadline `2027-09-01`, a quarter ahead of Europe.**
🟡 **Note the demand signal is uneven: Ipsos' 2026 Education Monitor shows support for banning AI
in schools RISING in Australia, Japan and South Korea while falling in Indonesia (`30%` → `23%`).**

### LATAM

🟢 **The gap is governance, and it is measured: `87%` of surveyed institutions use AI, `26%` have a
strategy; in Brazil `53%` of administrators use AI and `22%` have guidelines.** 🔵 **That ratio is
the engagement — institutional AI governance as a product, not a policy PDF: an inventory, a risk
register, an acceptable-use framework and a measurement plan.** 🟢 **UNESCO's regional observatory,
`Latam-GPT` and the `716`-instrument regulatory map give the work credible anchors.** 🔴 **And this
pass adds a supply-side caution: Spanish- and Portuguese-language education repositories returned
`0` results at every threshold tried, so the regional build must assume content is created, not
adopted** — see `compose/patterns.md` `P102`.

### Global

🔵 **Build the assessment-conformity artefact once.** 🟢 **The EU (`2027-12-02`) and Vietnam
(`2027-09-01`) converge on automated learner assessment as the regulated object from different
legal traditions; a portable conformity package — model documentation, human-review design,
disclosure surfaces, marking implementation — amortises across both and is reusable in every
jurisdiction that follows.** 🔴 **Carry the `45%` ESSA evidence figure into every pitch: the market
is tightening on proof, and most incumbents cannot show it.**

### 🟡 EMEA — the one new fact, and it is an operational one: the UAE has turned "which model does your tutor call?" into a procurement gate

🟡 **Secondary sourcing only — multiple outlets, consistent wording, and the
ministry's own text was NOT retrieved.** The UAE Ministry of Education has
issued a guide reported as *Safe and Responsible Use of Artificial Intelligence
in Classrooms 2026*, carrying what several outlets count as **`25`
prohibitions**. 🔴 **This base could not verify the count and does not assert
it.** What is consistently reported:

- 🔴 **Generative AI is prohibited for students under 13 / below Grade 7.**
- 🔴 **Prohibited in formal examinations and official assessments.**
- 🔴 **Unapproved generative-AI platforms are barred from classrooms**, with an
  **approved-platform annex** naming the permitted tools.
- 🟡 Use only under teacher supervision; copying or rephrasing AI output
  without demonstrated understanding is prohibited; content contradicting
  Islamic values, national identity or cultural ethics is prohibited.
- 🟡 Enforcement runs through the existing Student Conduct Management
  Regulations rather than a new penalty regime.

🔵 **Why this is a build constraint and not a news item.** An approved-platform
annex plus an age gate means a deployed tutor must be able to prove *which
model served this turn* and *how old this learner is*, per interaction. 🟢
**That favours the self-hostable, provider-neutral end of this base's shelf** —
`OpenMAIC` (MIT), `Kolibri` (MIT), Ollama-backed deployments — and penalises
anything that can only reach one hosted frontier endpoint.

🟢 **The rest of the EMEA leg corroborated shelved facts**: the `Digital
Omnibus` political agreement of 7 May 2026 and the high-risk-education date of
**2 December 2027** (this base already carries that date as a two-source
conflict and the conflict is unchanged); the Article 6 draft-guidelines
consultation that closed 23 June 2026; Council conclusions on teachers in the
era of AI, 11 May 2026; the Eurydice *Digital education at school in Europe
2026* report of 22 September 2026; Futuresource's **67% of teachers now using

expecting to use AI in their learning** and **80% of teachers worried AI is
entering classrooms faster than schools can manage**; Fosway's 2026 AI Market

### 🔵 North America — the legislative count is now a THREE-way conflict, and that is the sellable fact

🟡 Three trackers, three numbers, same 2026 session: **Multistate counts `134`
AI-in-education bills across `31` states**; **FutureEd counts roughly `71`
across `27`**; a third tracker reports **"nearly `100` state bills"** affecting
K-12 AI use, alongside an Education Commission of the States framing of **"at
least `15` states" having introduced or passed** AI-in-education legislation.
🔴 **Do not quote a single number.** 🔵 **Quote the spread** — it is itself the
market signal: a `134`-versus-`71` disagreement between two serious trackers
means the category boundary (what counts as an "AI in education" bill) is not
settled, and an institution cannot self-serve a compliance answer from it.

🟢 Corroborated and already shelved: Virginia `S.B. 394` as an enacted example
directing state guidance including teacher training; federal action running
through executive orders issued between 2025 and early 2026, producing a
national framework and a **K-12 AI task force** rather than statute; a
California ballot initiative announced January 2026 on AI interaction with
minors; the US Department of Education's **$169M FIPSE** awards; Instructure's
**Canvas Career**; Panorama Education's guiding principles developed with four
states. 🟡 Market framing: the US AI-in-education market at ~**$3.28B (2025)**
heading to ~**$15.25B (2031)**, with North America's share given as **38% of
the global market** by one source and **41.7% of forecast growth** by another —
a conflict, not a consensus.

### 🔵 LATAM — adoption at parity, governance at 30%, and the region where `T144` bites hardest

🟢 Corroborated: the Digital Education Council's 2026 LATAM survey —
**92% of students and 79% of faculty actively engaging with AI**, run with
Tecnológico de Monterrey 🟡 *on a sample of 29 universities*; UNESCO IESALC's
finding that adoption is "neither uniform nor evenly distributed", with a
private/public split; the UNU-IAS / IESALC working paper of September 2026; the

American universities have published a policy on how AI may be used**, against
92% student engagement. 🔵 A 62-point gap between practice and policy is a
governance engagement, not a tooling one.

🔴 **And LATAM is where this pass's blind spot is most expensive.** The single
Hugging Face model URL on this entire shelf — `CenIA/nllb-200-3.3B-spa-rap`,
Spanish–Rapa Nui translation — **is a LATAM asset.** The region's flagship open
contributions (`Latam-GPT`, CENIA's models) are *weights*, not repositories,
and this base's discovery battery ends every leg in the word `github`. 🟢
**A LATAM engagement served only from this shelf would be served the thinnest
slice of what the region has actually published.** → `T144`.

### 🔵 Global — the buyer's question is still evidence, and the supply numbers finally match the demand ones in rigour

🟡 Market size remains unresolved: **~$10.6B (2026) → $42.48B (2030)** at ~41.5%

🔴 **The hardest demand-side fact this pass**: only **45% of the most-used
edtech tools have published research meeting an ESSA evidence tier**, and the

### 🟢 Opportunities by region as measured at the forty-eighth pass (`p843`) — pass-scoped; the canonical per-region archive is the `## Opportunities by region` section below

#### North America
- 🟢 **Compliance-evidence layer.** The `134`/`~71`/`~100` bill-count spread
  means no institution can self-serve "are we compliant?". Sell the artefact
  that answers it: a per-jurisdiction policy map wired to the LMS seam
  (`canvas-mcp`, `D2L-MCP`, `brightspace-d2l-mcp` — all MIT, all byte-verified
  on this shelf) that logs which model served which learner interaction.
- 🟡 **Evidence-tier productisation.** With only 45% of top tools carrying ESSA-
  tier research, an engagement that ships an evaluation harness alongside the
  tutor is differentiated on the exact axis the buyer named.

#### EMEA
- 🔴 **Approved-platform + age-gate enforcement, driven by the UAE guide.**
  Build the gate, not the tutor: model-attribution per turn, an age/grade gate
  at Grade 7, exam-mode lockout, and an allowlist the ministry's annex can be
  mapped onto. Self-hostable stack (`OpenMAIC` MIT, `Kolibri` MIT, Ollama).
- 🟢 **A 13-month AI Act runway.** High-risk education obligations apply from
  **2 December 2027**; conformity work started in 2026 is early, not late.
- 🟡 **Sovereignty tooling has a forge dimension now** — the Dutch government's
  April 2026 Forgejo pilot — which strengthens the self-hosted argument beyond
  model hosting alone.

#### APAC
- 🟢 **Start from `OpenMAIC`, not from scratch.** MIT, 43,683★, Tsinghua
  provenance, pushed today, self-hostable. It is the strongest permissive
  starting point in this industry on any shelf this base keeps.
- 🔴 **Build licence literacy for Chinese-origin supply** (MulanPSL-2.0 and
  similar). A US-centric SPDX filter silently drops this supply, and APAC
  engagements are where that costs the most.
- 🟡 **Policy-calendar advisory**: the World Bank's Seoul academy and the
  national guidance wave give a convening hook into ministries.

#### LATAM
- 🔴 **Governance-first engagements.** 92% student engagement against ~30% of
  universities with a published policy is the clearest adoption/governance gap
  of the four regions. Sell policy, acceptable-use instrumentation and audit
  trails before tooling.
- 🟢 **Weights, not just repos.** Latam-GPT and CENIA's models are the region's
  real open assets; an engagement built only on GitHub-shelved repositories
  under-serves them. Add a model-evaluation workstream.
- 🟡 **Spanish/Portuguese-language delivery** remains a durable differentiator,
  and the one LATAM model asset this base holds is itself a low-resource
  translation model.

#### Global
- 🟢 **Shelf hygiene as a service, and this pass proves the need.** A
  first-party sweep of this base's own shelves found **2 dead agent rows, 6
  archived foundations (including `frappe/mcp`, the MIT ERPNext seam, archived
  last month), 39 foundations with no push in six months, and 3 licence claims
  that nothing had byte-read.** Any client running an open-source stack has the
  same rot and no instrument. `P95` is the instrument. → `compose/patterns.md`.

## 🟡 Forty-seventh pass (`p842`, 2026-11-16) — **`4` regions queried plus Global. The headline is a SATURATION measurement: `12` of `12` high-value regional facts this pass surfaced were ALREADY on this shelf.**

🔵 **Provenance discipline for this section: every demand-side and regulatory figure below comes from
web sources and is labelled 🟡 NOT primary-verified. Supply-side facts (refs, licences, file
inventories) were measured directly `2026-11-16` and are 🟢.**

🟢 **The saturation probe, stated first, because it changes how this file should be read.** After
running the four regional legs, each distinctive fact they produced was grepped against this base.
**All twelve were already present**: `NY A 9190`, North Carolina `Session Law 2026-41`, the Korean
`AI Basic Act`, `Latam-GPT`, UNESCO `IESALC`, the Pearson–`Workera` deal, `TEQSA`, the EU AI
`omnibus`, the `Digital Education Council` survey, the `134`-bill count, the UNESCO LATAM
`Observatorio`, and the `92%` student-adoption figure. 🔵 **This base's regional intel has reached
the point where an hourly pass re-derives it rather than extending it.** The honest consequence:
the four legs below are a **re-verification**, and their value is corroboration plus two surfaced
*conflicts*, not new ground.


### 🔵 North America — the regulation is sub-federal, the volume is still climbing, and this pass surfaces a COUNT CONFLICT the base should carry

🟡 Two trackers disagree on the size of the 2026 legislative wave, and both are secondary:
**Multistate** counts **`134` AI-in-education bills across `31` states**; **FutureEd** counts
**roughly `71` bills across `27` states** for the same session. 🔴 **Do not quote a single number to
a client — quote the range and the disagreement**, which is itself the more defensible point: the
volume is large and no one agrees on it.
🟡 Named instruments this leg returned: **`NY A 9190`** would restrict classroom AI to **grade 9 and
above**, excepting diagnostics and special-education interventions; **Maryland** requires state
guidance, local AI policies and AI coordinators; **North Carolina `Session Law 2026-41`** sets a
staged calendar — state model policy by **2026-12-31**, local policies by **2027-06-30**, teacher
training by **2028-06-30**. 🟡 One aggregator claims **`35+` states** now have education-department
AI guidance, and that the **US Department of Education** finalised a rule prioritising federal
grants for projects advancing understanding of AI or its ethical use in education.
🔵 **Opportunity:** the staged-deadline shape of NC's law is the sellable one — *model policy →
local policy → teacher training* is a three-phase delivery with public dates attached. Pair it with
the `P83` pedagogical acceptance gate so the training phase ships evidence, not slideware.

### 🟡 EMEA — the AI Act is enforcing, and the high-risk education date is STILL a two-source conflict (now reproduced independently)

🟡 The Commission's own policy page states the **AI Office and national authorities began enforcing
the AI Act on `2026-08-02`**, with an **"AI omnibus" amendment in force `2026-07-27`**.
🔴 **The education-specific high-risk date remains contested, and `p842` reproduced the conflict
from fresh sources rather than resolving it:** one source puts stand-alone high-risk obligations at
**`2027-12-02`** under the omnibus; another applies them from **August 2026**. 🟢 **Independent
reproduction of a conflict is worth recording — it means the ambiguity is in the public record, not
in this base's sourcing.** Sell against the earlier date; plan against the later one.
🟡 Education uses named high-risk by this leg: **admissions, grading, and cheating detection**, with
conformity assessment and human oversight obligations. 🟡 **UK DfE** published generative-AI product
safety standards for education (January 2026); **Ofqual** issued coursework-integrity resources
(March 2026). 🟡 In the Gulf, Dubai's **KHDA** has issued responsible-AI guidance to private schools.
🟡 Vendor movement: **OpenAI** reports national partnerships with **Estonia, Slovakia, Greece and
Italy**; **Pearson** agreed to acquire **Workera** for AI-skills verification; **Instructure** is
building an AI study coach into **Canvas**.
🔵 **Opportunity:** grading and admissions are the two high-risk categories *and* the two with the
most mature open-source integration layer on this shelf (`canvas-mcp` fork family, MIT). A
conformity-ready grading assistant — human oversight in the loop by construction — is the EMEA
engagement. 🔴 **Note the supply trap `p842` found: the two newest grading repos are unlicensed**
(`agents/trending.md`), so build from `delip/autorubric` / `macsnoeren/genai-open-assessment`.

### 🟢 APAC — the most binding regulatory calendar anywhere, and it names education explicitly

🟡 **South Korea** is the clearest binding case: the **AI Basic Act entered into force `2026-01-22`**
and treats **education as a high-impact domain**; enforcement decrees are reported still being
finalised, with a guidance-first grace period through 2026. 🟡 **Vietnam**'s regime classifies
high-risk systems including education and requires **pre-deployment registration**; one source dates
it `2026-03-01` 🔴 **but the sources do not agree on Vietnam's dates — treat as unconfirmed.**
🟡 **Japan** stays voluntary: AI Promotion Act (2025), AI Basic Plan (December 2025), AI Guidelines
for Business v1.2 (March 2026). 🟡 **Singapore** uses toolkits, not statute. 🟡 **Australia**:
proposed mandatory guardrails for high-risk uses including education, and **TEQSA has required all
higher-education providers to submit institutional action plans** on generative-AI risk. 🟡 **China**
is the most assertive — mandatory registration, content labelling, penalties reported up to **¥50M**.
🔴 **Declared gap, unchanged:** this leg returned **no education-specific adoption data for APAC** —
only regulation. The gap is in the public reporting, not in the querying; it has now persisted across
multiple passes.
🔵 **Opportunity:** TEQSA's institutional action plans are a procurement-shaped artefact with a
regulator as the audience. Korea's high-impact designation plus Vietnam's registration requirement
make **documentation generation** the APAC wedge — and `P91`'s notes already cover the delivery.

## 🟡 Forty-fifth pass (`p840`, 2026-11-16) — **`4` regions queried plus Global; the headline is that EMEA's high-risk education date has MOVED BY REGULATION, and this pass can finally name the instrument**

🔵 **Provenance discipline for this section: demand-side figures below come from web sources and are
labelled 🟡 NOT primary-verified unless named otherwise. Supply-side figures (stars, forks, licences,
refs) were measured directly `2026-11-16` and are 🟢.**

### 🟢 EMEA — the high-risk education deadline moved, and the transparency deadline did NOT

🟢 **The finding that changes the pitch: the education high-risk obligations are no longer an
August 2026 problem.** 🟡 **Multiple sources attribute the shift to the `2026` Digital Omnibus —
cited as `Regulation (EU) 2026/1744` — moving the EU AI Act's high-risk rules for education from
`August 2026` to `2 December 2027`.** 🔴 **Treat the regulation number as NOT primary-verified: it
comes from secondary compliance commentary, and this base has carried a two-source conflict on this
date for three consecutive passes. `p840` records that the conflict now resolves toward a DELAY, and
that the citation still needs EUR-Lex confirmation.**

🔴 **What did NOT move, and this is the sellable part:** 🟡 **Article 50 transparency obligations are
reported as un-postponed and applying from `2 August 2026`.** 🟢 **So the sequencing for an EMEA
engagement inverts the usual assumption: transparency first (weeks away), high-risk conformity
second (late 2027).** A roadmap that front-loads high-risk conformity work is selling the client the
wrong year.

🟢 **Already in force and absolute — the one bright line:** Article 5(1)(f) bans AI that infers
emotions in education institutions, **in force since February 2025**, with a narrow medical/safety
carve-out. 🔴 **Any engagement touching engagement-detection, attention-tracking or
affect-from-webcam in an EU classroom is prohibited work, not risky work.**

🟡 **High-risk scope, as the commentary describes it:** admissions, grading and learning-outcome
assessment, exam proctoring, plagiarism/AI-detection used *in assessment*, and personalised learning
systems that steer learning paths. 🟡 Schools are **deployers** under Article 4 and owe AI-literacy
**measures** — wording softened from guaranteeing a literacy level. 🟡 Commission draft guidance of
May 2026 is reported as treating much education AI as profiling, narrowing an exception.

🟢 **Supply, measured this pass:** EMEA produced the pass's only new agent row —
[`nurkan1/anatria-3d`](https://github.com/nurkan1/anatria-3d) — and 🔴 **it is `BSL 1.1`, usable in
teaching but not in a revenue-generating deployment before `2030-10-30`.** 🔵 EMEA's open-source
education supply remains thin relative to its regulatory sophistication: **the region's demand is
compliance engineering, not tooling.**

### 🔵 North America — the regulation is sub-federal, and the volume is still the story

🟡 **`134` AI-in-education bills across `31` states in the 2026 session** (legislative tracker, NOT

🟡 **Named measures worth quoting in a proposal:** **California AB 1159** — bars using student data

governance into three dated deliverables a district must produce, which is a scope, not a sentiment.

🟡 Federally: Department of Education guidance and an Office of Educational Technology framework in
2026; an NEPC risk-tiered framework approved May 2026 pending comment; a K-12 AI Literacy and

rollout is a `50`-jurisdiction problem and should be priced as one.**

🟡 **South Korea's AI Basic Act in force `22 January 2026`** — reported as the region's first

naming automated assessment**, with **`12`-month transitions generally and `18` months for

content for grades `1`–`12`.**

AI-in-classroom pitch there faces majority ban sentiment and should lead with governance, not

coordination), Dubai KHDA guidance to private schools.

| Jurisdiction | Instrument | Education-specific effect |
|---|---|---|
| **South Korea** | **AI Basic Act, in force `2026-01-22`** | 🔴 **Names education as a sector where "high-impact AI" carries extra obligations.** Enforcement decrees reportedly still being finalised, with a guidance-focused grace period through 2026. |
| **Vietnam** | **Law on Artificial Intelligence, in force `2026-03-01`** | 🔴 **AI in education treated as HIGH-RISK: pre-deployment registration in the National AI Database plus human oversight.** The most prescriptive education-specific requirement measured in any region. |
| **Japan** | AI Promotion Act (2025), AI Basic Plan (Dec 2025), AI Guidelines for Business v1.2 (Mar 2026) | 🟢 Voluntary guidance posture. |
| **Singapore** | No binding statute; **AI Verify** toolkit | 🟢 Testing-framework posture. |
| **Australia** | 2024 mandatory-guardrails proposal covering education; **TEQSA** | 🔴 **TEQSA has required ALL higher-education providers to submit institutional action plans addressing generative-AI risks** — a live, dated compliance artefact. |
| **China** | Algorithm and content rules | 🟡 Detailed but not education-specific. |

🔵 **Spillover, and it cuts both ways:** APAC edtech selling proctoring or admissions tools into EU
schools falls inside the AI Act's education category.

🟢 **The supply, measured:** `inno-agent` (China, **`1,497★`**, MIT — a three-layer learner-memory
architecture, the first on these shelves whose design centre is a learner model rather than a chat
history); `AI-Agent-Literacy` (**Taiwan, NTNU-piloted**, 🔴 code MIT but content **CC BY-NC-SA**, so
read the pedagogy and do not ship the content); `awesome-agentic-ai-zh` (**`8,782★`**, MIT, trilingual
— one AI-literacy sequence serving Taipei, Singapore and mainland cohorts with no translation
project); `dual-sub-replay` (Vietnam, MIT, **on-device** translation); `Learnova` (India, `239` forks
against `96★`); Thai school administration (`30` forks against `26★`, nine years maintained).
🟢 **Three of these have fork-to-star ratios at or above `1:1` — the deployment signal.**

🟢 **Opportunity:** Vietnam's pre-deployment registration requirement and Korea's high-impact
designation make APAC the region where a **compliance-first delivery** is not a differentiator but
an entry condition — and `inno-agent`'s stance that "durable facts go to tools, not replies", with an
inspectable, learner-editable profile and unevidenced labels forbidden, is a traceability
architecture that maps directly onto a human-oversight requirement. 🔴 **Its declared non-goals —
one active session, no auth model, no tenant isolation — mean an institutional deployment is a fleet
of single-tenant instances. Price that.**

### 🔵 LATAM — adoption is at parity with the North, governance is not, and this pass finally has something build-safe to offer

🟢 **The demand picture is the most strongly sourced of any region this pass, and it is consistent
with `p837`/`p838`.** **UNESCO IESALC**, surveying **`200` higher-education institutions across `19`
countries**: **`87%` use AI in at least one area of activity, while only `26%` hold a formal AI
strategy.** A **Digital Education Council** LATAM survey: **`92%` of students and `79%` of faculty**
actively engaging with AI. In schools, **more than `50%` of teachers in countries such as Chile and
Brazil** already use these tools, while **fewer than `10%` of institutions in the region have formal
guidelines.**

🟡 **Regulation is volume without convergence.** A 2026 **Universidad de los Andes** study counted
**`716` AI-related regulatory instruments, rules and legislative proposals across `23` countries and
territories**, most of them recent. 🔵 Commentary argues the region should pursue common definitions,
risk categories, audit principles and mutual-recognition mechanisms rather than copy the US, EU or
Chinese models wholesale.

🔵 **Institutional players to engage, named:** **UNESCO IESALC** (with UNU-IAS); **UNESCO**'s
regional **Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean**, launched in
**Santiago, April 2026**, hosted by **ECLAC**; **CENIA** (Chile); **Tecnológico de Monterrey** and
its Institute for the Future of Education; **Ceibal** (Uruguay) and **Cetic.br** (Brazil) for
national usage data; **Universidad de los Andes** (Bogotá) for regulatory tracking.

🟢 **The supply finding, and it closes a gap this base has declared for four straight passes.**

intersected with a permissive licence and a recent push window return `0`, with controls run and the
search rate limit read at `30/30` to prove the instrument was alive.** 🟢 **But the region publishes
licence-clean national education DATA, engineered as infrastructure:** `chile-hub` (`139★`, code

attribution only, no share-alike, no non-commercial clause** — as Parquet for Polars/DuckDB, joined
to normalised `CUT` codes, with per-layer provenance, drift detection, fail-loud CI and a Zenodo DOI.
🔵 **Plus `FAIR`, whose documentation is published in Spanish as well as English — the most
LATAM-ready assessment row on these shelves.**

🟢 **The opportunity, and the sequencing inverts the usual one:** against `87%` adoption and `26%`
strategy, the region's buyer is not asking for another tutor — they are asking what their AI is
doing and whether it is working. 🟢 **So open with the EVIDENCE layer on national open data, not with
an agent, because the evidence layer is the thing that is build-safe here today.** → `P90`.

🔴 **Declared residual gap, so silence is not read as coverage: licence-clean education data was
verified for CHILE and for no other LATAM country. Brazil (INEP), Mexico (SEP), Colombia (ICFES) and
Argentina all publish national education data, and `p839` verified neither their licence terms nor

### 🔵 Global — the buyer's question is evidence, and the instruments to answer it now exist on these shelves

🟡 **Market size remains contested and is labelled as such:** AI in education at **`$10.4B`**
(2026) in one source, **`$11.4B`** in another, **`$12.3B` by 2026** (HolonIQ) in a third. 🔴 **These
are different definitions, not different measurements of one thing — do not average them.**

🟢 **Adoption:** **`86%` of higher-education students globally** report using AI in their studies
(Digital Education Council).

🔵 **The consistent theme across every source this pass: the shift from experiment to infrastructure,
and with it a shift from personalisation promises to PROVEN instructional benefit.** The OECD's 2026
Digital Education Outlook reportedly favours purpose-built educational AI designed for durable
learning gains over general-purpose tools; universities are deploying agents for admission queries,
document verification, exam scheduling and evaluation workflows; AI literacy is becoming a core
skill and digital credentials are gaining traction.

🟢 **This base can now answer that question with measured artefacts rather than argument:**

CorrectExam keep the GRADE deterministic and generate only the feedback; and the
`ai-infra-curriculum` estate plus `awesome-agentic-ai-zh` supply the AI-literacy sequence the market
is now buying. 🔵 **`P91` wires the literacy programme; `P90` wires the evidence layer.**

## 🟢 Forty-second pass (`p837`, 2026-11-16) — **`4` regions queried, the EU high-risk date is no longer contested but the INSTRUMENT to confirm it is unreachable, and Indonesia joins the measured no-local-supply list**

🔵 **Queries run: the four mandated global legs, the four mandated regional legs (North America,
EMEA, APAC, LATAM), plus `EU AI Act Digital Omnibus high-risk obligations delayed December 2027
education` and two non-English legs (Japanese, Indonesian).**

### 🟡 EMEA — the contested EU date, and why this pass does NOT close it

🔴 **`p836` left line-level flags in this file saying the EU high-risk application date is
CONTESTED between `2026-08-02` and `2027-12-02`, and told readers not to sell against a single
date. 🟢 This pass found the mechanism that reconciles them, and could not reach the primary
source.**

- 🟢 **The mechanism, consistent across every secondary source read this pass:** the **Digital
  Omnibus** deferred **Annex III standalone** high-risk obligations — which is the limb education
  sits in (admissions, assessment, proctoring, learning-outcome prediction) — from **`2026-08-02`
  to `2027-12-02`**, and **Annex I** product-embedded systems from `2027-08-02` to **`2028-08-02`**.
- 🟢 **What did NOT move, and this is the part a compliance package gets wrong:** **Article 50
  transparency duties have applied since `2026-08-02`** and were untouched; the Omnibus **added two
  new Article 5 prohibitions phasing in `2026-12-02`**. 🔴 **So "the EU delayed the AI Act" is
  false for an education deployment: the transparency obligations are live NOW, and a new
  prohibition lands in a fortnight.**
- 🟡 **One source read this pass states the instrument as `Regulation (EU) 2026/1744`, published in
  the Official Journal `2026-07-24` and in force `2026-07-27`.** 🔴 **NOT CORROBORATED, and
  recorded as uncorroborated:** no second independent source confirmed the OJ citation, and an
  earlier source in the same result set reports the second trilogue of `2026-04-28` ending
  **without** agreement (plausibly superseded, not demonstrably).
- 🔴 **`eur-lex.europa.eu` DOES NOT RESOLVE from this session** — the host is outside the
  session's allowed network domains. 🟢 **That is an INSTRUMENT limitation, stated rather than
  worked around: this base cannot currently read EU primary law, so every AI Act date here is
  secondary-sourced by construction.** 🟡 **`ACTION p837-B`: allow `eur-lex.europa.eu` in the
  session network policy, then verify `Regulation (EU) 2026/1744` against the OJ. Until then the
  `2027-12-02` date is reported as STRONGLY CORROBORATED SECONDARY, not as law read at source.**

🔵 **Other EMEA intelligence this pass:** **Ofqual published principles for AI use in high-stakes
marking in England** — 🟢 **the first named regulator position on MACHINE MARKING in this base, and
it lands directly on `T96`/`P84`**. **Futuresource**: `67%` of teachers now using AI tools, with
AI PCs projected at ~`15%` of education PC shipments this year. **Epson (UK, FR, IT, DE, ES, PL)**:
`76%` of students expect to be able to use AI in their learning, while `80%` of teachers worry AI
is entering the classroom faster than schools can manage. 🔵 **The European Commission's Eurydice
report *Digital education at school in Europe 2026*** and **Fosway's 2026 AI Market Assessment**
(vendor-facing, EMEA learning leaders) are the two reference documents a European proposal is
expected to have read. The **Digital Education Council EMEA summit** (Paris) convened leaders from
universities across `14` countries. 🟡 **The UAE's `25` generative-AI prohibitions for schools
(February 2026) — age suitability, privacy, academic integrity, exams — are recorded HERE, under
EMEA, although the leg that surfaced them was the APAC leg.**

### 🔵 Global — the buyer's question changed, and it changed in this base's favour

🟢 **Education is reported as having left the generative-AI "panic phase": attention has moved from
policing academic integrity to reducing cognitive load, with the OECD's *Digital Education Outlook

credentialing, full-classroom VR, and the chatbot-as-tutor pitch.**

## 🟢 Forty-first pass (`p836`, 2026-11-15) — **`4` regions queried, `1` four-pass-old gap RESOLVED by corroboration, `1` date this base must stop selling against**

🟢 **The four regional queries were re-run and, unlike `p835`, this pass is not a null: the ADA
Title II dates that `intel/open-gaps.md` has carried as UNRESOLVED for four passes are now
corroborated, and they are NOT the dates a compliance package would have assumed.**

### 🟢 RESOLVED — `574-E`: the ADA Title II web rule was EXTENDED BY ONE YEAR

🔴 **The finding: the deadline this base and the wider market have been planning against moved,
and it moved in April 2026.**

- **Interim final rule issued `2026-04-20`** by the US Department of Justice, **amending
  `28 CFR 35.200(b)`.**
- **Public entities serving ≥ `50,000` people: `2027-04-26`** (previously `2026-04-24`).
- **Smaller entities and special district governments: `2028-04-26`** (previously `2027-04-26`).
- 🟢 **The technical standard did NOT change: `WCAG 2.1 Level AA` stands**, and the standing Title
  II duty to make services accessible applies regardless of the dated web rule.
- 🟡 Comment period reported as running through **`2026-06-22`**; the rule is also reported as
  effective immediately. 🔵 **Those are compatible (an interim final rule takes effect while
  comments run), but the exact effective date is unconfirmed here.**
- 🔵 DOJ's stated rationale, reported consistently: it **overestimated entities' staffing and
  technology capacity.** Commentators note DOJ has signalled further review of the digital
  accessibility requirements, with **no new technical standard adopted.**

🟢 **Evidence posture, stated honestly: this is SIX independent secondary corroborations, not a
primary read.** Reed Smith, Jackson Lewis, the Consumer Financial Services Law Monitor,
accessible.org, UPCEA and the Title II Center / AudioEye all report the same rule date, the same
CFR citation and the same two dates. 🔴 **The primary remains unreachable from this session, and
the blocker is now better characterised than the base recorded it:** `federalregister.gov`,
`ecfr.gov`, `govinfo.gov` and `ada.gov` all return **`CONNECT tunnel failed, response 403`** via
`curl` (an egress ALLOW-LIST refusal at the agent proxy) and **`getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND`** via the
fetch tool. 🔵 **The base had recorded only the `ENOTFOUND` surface; both are the same egress
denial seen through two different instruments.** 🟡 **`574-E` moves from UNRESOLVED to
CORROBORATED-NOT-PRIMARY**, and it should stay that way until a `.gov` domain is allowed.

🔴 **Consequence for `P-577-B`, which `intel/open-gaps.md` flagged as "selling a compliance package
against dates no primary confirms":** 🟢 **the dates it should quote are `2027-04-26` and
`2028-04-26`, with the standard unchanged at `WCAG 2.1 AA`.** 🔴 **A proposal quoting April 2026 is
now wrong by a year — and the commercial read is the opposite of relief: the extension buys a
remediation programme time to be done properly, and it removes the excuse for a
checkbox-and-overlay deliverable.**

🔵 **One conflation to refuse explicitly.** A secondary source read this pass gives the original
large-entity date as **`2026-04-22`**. 🔴 **This base's `2026-04-22` is the updated **COPPA**
compliance date — a different rule entirely** — and the 2024 Title II rule's own date was
`2026-04-24`. 🟢 **Two adjacent April 2026 dates for two different statutes; they are not the same
obligation and must not be merged in a client deck.** 🟡 One source also notes an **HHS deadline in
May 2026 that the DOJ extension does NOT move**; its scope for education was not established this
pass and is **not** recorded as an education obligation.

  and algorithm filing before public launch, content labelling in force since **`2025-09-01`**, and
  **measures on anthropomorphic AI services effective `2026-07-15`** (directly relevant to a tutor
  or AI-companion product). **India** governs through amended IT Rules — synthetic content must
  carry prominent labels and provenance markers from **`2026-02-20`** — with dedicated AI
  legislation only *signalled* as of July 2026. **Japan**'s AI Promotion Act remains
  promotion-first with no direct penalties; a second AI Basic Plan was approved in July.
- **LATAM.** Re-confirmed: **Brazil's PL 2338/2023** remains the region's first comprehensive,
  risk-based AI bill and is **still not enacted** — education is a covered use case, so this stays
  a watch item and must not be quoted as law. **Mexico** has **no enacted AI statute**, with an
  explicit *technological sovereignty* framing. 🟢 **Market structure: HolonIQ's `2026` LATAM
  EdTech 100 puts Brazil at nearly half the cohort** and most deal activity; named AI-native
  players include **Teachy** (BR, AI lesson preparation, now expanding into Asia), **Vetto** (BR)
  and **Ginia** (MX) on employability, with **Mattilda** (MX) in school billing and **Talisis**
  (MX) in post-secondary. 🔵 **Education *financing* is now among the region's largest categories
  while learning platforms lost the most ground** — the buyer is increasingly the bursar, not the
  pedagogy office. An **IDB** review of **`193`** initiatives found **`59%`** used generative AI,
  **`27%`** language models and **~`48%`** combined modalities, concluding the region builds
  hybrid, context-sensitive systems rather than importing them.

🔴 **Contested figure, flagged not resolved.** `2026` AI-in-education market size spreads across
**`$6.4B`–`$11.4B`** depending on the firm, with onward projections to **`$42.48B` by 2030
(`41.5%` CAGR)** or **`$32.27B` by 2030 (`31.2%` CAGR)**. 🔵 **A separate and more decision-relevant
signal points the other way: venture funding fell to `~$1B` in H1 `2026`, down `26%` YoY** — usage
is rising while investor appetite cools, which is the condition in which open-source substitution
wins budget. 🔴 **No single market figure should be quoted as settled in a proposal.**

intelligence and **no new regionally placed open-source software** in this window. `p835` therefore

## Market size — contested

Four 2026 figures for the AI-in-education market, all for the same year:
**$10.4B**, **$11.4B**, **$12.3B** (HolonIQ), and projections running from
$32.3B (by 2030) to $57B (by 2033). All trace to secondary vendor sources. Use
"roughly $10–12B in 2026, growing fast" and do not quote a specific figure to a
client without a primary report.

**Segment figures added 2026-11-13 (third pass).** The whole-market number is
the least useful one to carry into a conversation; the segment numbers are
narrower and better matched to what a studio actually sells:

| Segment | 2026 | Forecast | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Agentic AI in education** | **$2.37B** | $11.83B by 2031 | Mordor Intelligence |
| **AI tutors** | **$2.7B** | $17.7B by 2033 | Grand View Research |
| AI in education (whole) | $11.4B | 25.9% CAGR 2026–2033 | Grand View Research |

Mordor also puts **Intelligent Tutoring Systems at 26.04% of the agentic
education segment (2025)** — the largest single share, which is consistent with
ITS being where the open-source agent supply in this KB is concentrated
(`DeepTutor`, `OATutor`, `tutor-mcp`, `ai-shifu`).

**Use the agentic figure, not the headline figure.** "$2.37B in 2026 growing to
$11.83B by 2031" is defensible, segment-correct and roughly 5× growth; "$11.4B
AI in education" includes hardware, smart classrooms and proctoring that no
studio engagement touches. Same caveat as above: all of these are secondary
vendor estimates, cited as such.

## The structural fact of this market

**Adoption has outrun governance everywhere, and the gap is the commercial
opportunity.** Every region shows the same shape — very high individual use by
students and teachers, very low institutional policy coverage:

| Evidence | Figure | Source type |
|---|---|---|
| Students using AI in their studies (16 countries) | 86% | Digital Education Council survey, via secondary |
| LATAM higher-ed institutions using AI in ≥1 area (200 institutions, 19 countries) | 87% | UNESCO IESALC study |
| LATAM institutions with any formal AI framework | 26% | UNESCO IESALC study |
| LATAM universities with no specific AI budget | 55% | UNESCO IESALC study |
| LATAM institutions that evaluate their AI implementation | 9% | UNESCO IESALC study |
| Brazilian school administrators using AI | 53% | Cetic.br |
| Brazilian schools with any AI guideline | 22% | Cetic.br |
| US faculty reporting a formal institutional AI policy | ~25% | secondary roundup |

The sellable work is therefore rarely "build a tutor". It is **governance,
integration, evaluation and teacher enablement around tools the institution is
already using unofficially.**

## Global players

**Open-source / buildable layer:** HKUDS (DeepTutor, Apache-2.0, 58.7k),
Tsinghua MAIC (OpenMAIC, MIT, 43.4k), JuneYaooo (teacher-to-skills,
Apache-2.0, 475), Open edX nonprofit (Harvard/MIT lineage), Moodle HQ,
Instructure (Canvas), Learning Equality (Kolibri, MIT), Khan Academy (Perseus
MIT; Khanmigo commercially), Oppia Foundation, Frappe, OpenEduCat, and the
LTI 1.3 maintainers (Cvmcosta/ltijs Apache-2.0; Packback; Simon
Initiative/Open Learning Initiative).

**Policy and standards setters:** UNESCO and UNESCO IESALC, OECD, European
Commission (AI Act, Digital Education Action Plan), 1EdTech (LTI, Caliper,
QTI), national education ministries.

**Commercial incumbents to integrate with or displace:** Instructure, Anthology,
PowerSchool, Google for Education, Microsoft Education, Duolingo, Chegg,
Coursera, 2U/edX, plus OpenAI and Anthropic distributing directly into
education — OpenAI with named government and education-system partnerships
across EMEA.

### North America

#### Update 2026-11-16 (fifty-second pass, `p847`) — 🟡 **the federal bill is still a bill; the opportunity is the CLASSROOM-COMPUTE line item, and it is permissively licensed**

🔴 **Status correction to carry into every US deck:** the `K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026`
**advanced in committee and has not passed**. 🟢 **The binding layer remains state law** — Idaho
`SB 1227`, Maryland `SB 720`, Ohio's July 2026 district-policy deadline, California `AB 1159`,
`134` bills across `31` states, all already held here.

🟢 **What `p847` adds is buildable.** A US district or faculty engagement that has to grade code,
run labs, or teach data skills now has a filed, permissive substrate it can own outright:
[`the-littlest-jupyterhub`](https://github.com/jupyterhub/the-littlest-jupyterhub) (`BSD-3-Clause`,
`1,147★`, scoped to `1–100` users on one server) under `P84` / `P89`, with
[`ryukzak/wrench`](https://github.com/ryukzak/wrench) (`BSD-3-Clause`) as the worked example of
deterministic auto-grading where only the FEEDBACK is generated. 🔵 **Deterministic grading is also
the cheapest answer to a state statute that restricts AI from making the evaluative decision:
the machine runs the program, the rubric is code, and the model only writes the explanation.**

🔴 **And one line item to remove from the pipeline:** the `4GeeksAcademy` / `learnpack` estate is
**not open source** (`agents/top.md`, Finding `3`). Any US curriculum proposal that assumed that
courseware could be forked must be re-costed. → `T159`.

🔴 **Still uncovered:** `0` sources on Canada in this pass's North America leg. A Canadian
engagement cannot be priced from this shelf.

#### Update 2026-11-16 (fifty-first pass, `p846`) — 🔴 **NIL on instruments a seventh time. The opportunity this pass creates is a LICENCE-SCANNER line item, not a new fact.**

🔴 **All `10` North American instruments the leg returned are held** (NC `SL 2026-41`, the `134`

bid.** 🔴 **Sakai — the one permissive LMS of scale in this market — is `ECL-2.0`, not
`Apache-2.0`.** A US university's open-source policy and its SPDX scanner enumerate permitted
licences by NAME. 🔵 **`ECL-2.0` is Apache-2.0 with a narrowed, institution-scoped patent grant;
it is OSI-approved and it will still be flagged as unrecognised by a scanner configured for
`Apache-2.0`.**

🟢 **The sellable position:** arrive at a North American higher-education engagement with the
licence annex already correct — `ECL-2.0` named and explained as a university-sector licence,
`1EdTech/lti-1-3-php-library` cited for LTI certification provenance, and the OpenWallet Foundation
stewardship of `learner-credential-wallet` cited for the credentialing layer. 🔵 **This is a
two-page annex that removes a procurement round-trip, and the measurement behind it is
first-party.** → `T151`, Finding `7`.

🔴 **Scope it honestly:** this is a licence-hygiene differentiator, not a product. It wins the
review, it does not win the deal.

#### Update 2026-11-15 (thirty-ninth pass, `p834`) — 🟢 **The CANADA and MEXICO blank is CLOSED at the policy layer, and in Canada the instrument is PROCUREMENT rather than legislation**

🔴 **`p833` named this blank and `p810` opened it: two countries inside a region this base places

🇨🇦 **CANADA — the finding is that there is no AI statute and that this does not mean there is no
rule.**
- 🔴 **Federal AI legislation is paused, not dead.** `AIDA` — which this base already names — fell
  with prorogation; sources describing it as "pending" are stale.
- 🟢 **NEW to this base: the Treasury Board `Directive on Automated Decision-Making`.** It binds
  federal institutions only, **but it has become procurement-relevant**, and public-sector
  procurement is described as emerging as a **de facto standard-setting mechanism** in the absence
  of a statute.
- 🟡 **NEW: `Bill C-34`, the Safe Social Media Act**, which would classify some AI systems used by
  children for learning as **regulated online services**. 🔴 **Its status could not be confirmed
  from the sources returned and is NOT carried as law.**
- 🔴 **The national AI-literacy initiative barely acknowledges K-12**, per a policy analysis
  arguing that a digital-safety regulator or the Privacy Commissioner should set national EdTech
  **procurement** standards.

🇲🇽 **MEXICO — no statute, and explicitly no procurement standard either.**
- 🔴 **No federal AI law.** AI is regulated indirectly through general law. The federal
  `Ley Nahui` bill remains in discussion (already held in `agents/trending.md`).
- 🔴 **NEW and load-bearing: Mexico has enacted NO AI-specific procurement or contracting
  standards.** 🔵 **That is the opposite of the Canadian position and it changes the sale: in
  Canada the conformance pack is the wedge, in Mexico there is nothing to conform to and the wedge
  is the institution's own policy.**
- 🟢 Already held and re-confirmed: the Estado de México reform of Article `61` of its education
  law (April `2026`, responsible and gradual AI use in upper-secondary and higher education), and
  the SEP's `ENIAG` survey with **more than `1.5M` student responses** — which this base had only
  in `intel/open-gaps.md` and which belongs here as the region's largest primary demand dataset.

`31`-states count, Maryland, Ohio's `2026-07-01` district deadline and the `K-12 AI Literacy and
Readiness Act of 2026` (`H.R. 8747`) are all on these shelves. 🟢 **NEW this pass: North
Carolina's dated cascade — a state model policy by `2026-12-31` and local policies by
`2027-06-30`; Utah `HB 218`'s required grade `7`/`8` digital-skills course including AI literacy;
the Department of Labor's `AI Literacy Framework` (February `2026`, voluntary); and AI literacy
made a priority across the Department of Education's discretionary grants.**

🔵 **The opportunity, and it is the same shape `p832` found in the US one layer down:** in North
America the buyer's obligation arrives as a **procurement clause or a dated policy deadline**, not
as a product requirement. 🟢 **`P74`'s override log is the artefact that satisfies both** — it is

and a Maryland district policy each separately ask a vendor to demonstrate.

🔴 **The standing North America blank is unchanged and is named again here: CANADA and MEXICO.**

instrument surfaced at all.** 🔵 **For a region this base places engagements in, a two-country
